The NTSB notes that communication delay leaves unclear whether that slowdown began before the operator disengaged the ADS at \(\ImpactInstant-0.02\text{ s}\) (National Transportation Safety Board 2019).

Recognition came seconds after detection; suppression then spent 18.7 m with the brake withheld.

6 Jerk Limit: Jerk, met in the stopping notebook as the rate of change of deceleration, is measured in m/s³. A jerk limit makes a brake ramp its deceleration up instead of stepping it, which spares occupants, loads, and transmissions a shock but costs stopping distance, because full deceleration arrives later. The warehouse machine’s card carries a jerk bound for the same reason, and the machine’s finished stopping budget pays for the ramp.

The first term of the warehouse stopping budget applies directly. Averaging the two recorded speeds over the 1 s of suppression, the vehicle traveled about 18.7 m while emergency braking was withheld, distance that no later action could recover. The speed still fell during that second because a slowdown for the planned right turn at the next intersection was already under way; what the suppression withheld was the emergency response (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). The emergency itself was defined by a chosen limit. The ADS treated a situation as an emergency when avoiding a collision required more than 7 m/s² (0.71 g) of deceleration or a jerk6 beyond its limit, and it capped its own automated braking at that deceleration. Police determined that the vehicle could decelerate at an average of 0.92 g, about 9.0 m/s², on that road surface (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). The difference between a chosen permission limit and a physical one is the distinction the book’s numbers keep throughout. The calculation stops short of a counterfactual stop, because the report does not establish when a different design would have begun braking. Detection, correct path prediction, hazard recognition, and physical braking are distinct events; reporting the first detection time alone conceals the failures between them.

↪ The stopping envelope, which charges every interval of delayed braking against the clear distance, is developed in Stopping Envelopes.

War Story 1.1: The disabled safety redundancy (2018)
Context: On March 18, 2018, a developmental ADS on a 2017 Volvo XC90 struck and killed a pedestrian crossing a road in Tempe, Arizona (National Transportation Safety Board 2019).

Mechanism: As traced above, classification changes discarded the object’s tracking history, and the one-second suppression withheld braking until only a gradual slowdown was planned. The design did not use maximum braking solely to mitigate an unavoidable collision (National Transportation Safety Board 2019).

Impact: The vehicle struck and fatally injured the pedestrian. The late gradual-slowdown plan could not recover the lost stopping opportunity.

Response: The NTSB determined that the probable cause was the vehicle operator’s failure to monitor the driving environment and the ADS because she was visually distracted throughout the trip by her personal cell phone. It found three contributing factors at Uber ATG, all a consequence of an inadequate safety culture: inadequate safety risk assessment procedures, ineffective oversight of vehicle operators, and a lack of adequate mechanisms for addressing operators’ automation complacency. The impaired pedestrian’s crossing outside a crosswalk and the Arizona Department of Transportation’s insufficient oversight of automated vehicle testing also contributed. The NTSB further found that deactivating the Volvo collision warning and automatic emergency braking, which Uber ATG had done over possible radar interference, removed a layer of safety redundancy, and it recommended stronger safety risk management for testing automated vehicles (National Transportation Safety Board 2019).

Systems lesson: Classification uncertainty, a delayed response rule, and reliance on an inattentive operator can combine. The design made the operator the primary countermeasure in an emergency, yet no mechanism monitored her attention in real time: authority rested on evidence nobody collected. A design needs timely hazard assessment and a response that can mitigate impact when avoidance is no longer possible; the investigation does not prescribe a particular hardware architecture.

7 Safety-Critical Standards: Three standards divide the ground. ISO 26262 addresses hazards caused by faults in a vehicle’s electrical and electronic systems (International Organization for Standardization 2018); ISO 21448, safety of the intended functionality (SOTIF), addresses hazards that arise when a function works as designed but performs insufficiently, as perception can (International Organization for Standardization 2022); and UL 4600 sets out the safety case for autonomous products with no human driver to fall back on (UL Standards & Engagement 2023). Tempe falls on the SOTIF side, since the recorded failures were in classification and path prediction rather than in a faulted component, and none of the three prescribes a processor architecture. The standards map for the running machine maps the standards that govern the running machine.

International Organization for Standardization. 2018. ISO 26262:2018: Road Vehicles, Functional Safety. International Organization for Standardization.
International Organization for Standardization. 2022. ISO 21448:2022: Road Vehicles, Safety of the Intended Functionality. International Organization for Standardization.

The investigation supports a broader architectural requirement. A developmental ADS needs safety redundancy and a timely mitigation path even when perception or path prediction fails, and simulation tests by Volvo that the NTSB cites suggest that the factory emergency braking might have prevented or mitigated this collision. The report does not establish when a separate permission path would have triggered or where it would have stopped the vehicle.7

Tempe illustrates failure before impact, where classification changes and a software suppression delay prevented timely braking. The 2023 Cruise robotaxi incident marks the opposite pole after impact. A hit-and-run driver’s car threw a pedestrian into the path of the driverless vehicle, which braked hard but struck her and stopped. It then began a pullover toward a minimal-risk condition8 and, because its sensing did not detect a person beneath the vehicle, moved about 20 ft (6.1 m) at up to 7.7 mph while she was pinned beneath it. This account comes from a review commissioned by the company’s boards, whose focus was the company’s disclosures to regulators (Quinn Emanuel Urquhart and Sullivan, LLP 2024).

8 Minimal-Risk Condition: In automated driving, the stable, usually stopped, state a system brings a vehicle to when a trip cannot or should not continue. Reaching it can itself require motion, as a pullover to the curb does, and in the Cruise case that motion was the harm. A fallback trajectory therefore needs the same permission check as any other proposal, judged against fresh evidence about the space it will move through.

↪ The Cruise pullover, and the fallback rule that forbids renewed motion while the space beneath the vehicle is unverified, are developed in The four rungs of the fallback ladder.

Neither failure reduces to an inaccurate model. The first spent stopping distance before braking began, and no later action could recover it. The second chose motion after the impact without fresh evidence about the space it moved through. Together they show what must hold before the machine acts and what a fallback may do once harm has begun. Both also had to be reconstructed afterward from records of what the machine perceived and when, so accountability, like safety, depends on the path that authorizes motion.

↪ Who answered for the Cruise collision, and the decisions behind an admitted action that no per-tick log records, are developed in Who Answered for the Cruise Collision.

Checkpoint 1.2: Forensics of boundary and authority failures

Verify how the Tempe mechanisms connect to the architecture:

1.9 Where Safety, Security, and Accountability Meet

Tempe and Cruise both turned on what the machine knew when it moved or failed to brake, and both had to be reconstructed afterward from what it had recorded. The same three questions (whether the evidence behind a motion was sound, whether anything could refuse it, and whether the decision can be traced) also describe an attack and an audit. In enterprise software and distributed systems, safety, security, and accountability usually live in separate tiers. Security defends network boundaries with cryptographic tokens, reliability frameworks mitigate component failures through retries and circuit breakers, and accountability records transactions to append-only databases. In physical AI, all three converge on the causal boundary, the interface where software commands meet physical actuators. Consider the running machine crossing a warehouse aisle. It needs a valid stopping decision whether its forward cameras are obscured by dirt, an attacker has injected spoofed packets onto its internal Controller Area Network (CAN) bus, or a vision-language-action policy has hallucinated an unobstructed path. In each case the hazard is kinetic, a collision with racking or a worker. Because momentum cannot be rolled back like a failed database transaction, admission control cannot simply confirm who dispatched a command. It must determine whether executing that command now would violate the machine’s physical limits.

An ordinary environmental change challenges this boundary as directly as an attack. The running machine’s credible deceleration of 2 m/s² rests on the premise, stated on its card, that the floor supplies a friction coefficient of at least 0.204. When the floor cannot supply the friction the brakes ask for, traction rather than the brakes caps deceleration, and braking distance grows in inverse proportion to the available friction. A film of spilled hydraulic fluid can drop tire-floor friction from about \(0.60\) on the aisle’s dry concrete to \(0.05\) (an illustrative pair). A vehicle that brakes at the traction limit would see its braking distance grow by a factor of 12. The running machine asks far less of a dry floor than it supplies, but \(0.05\) falls below its premise. Its credible deceleration therefore no longer holds, and every clearance envelope and speed profile computed from that deceleration a moment earlier is invalid. The learned planner, processing camera streams that do not reveal the transparent slick, may continue to output an aggressive velocity command with high nominal confidence. Safety verification cannot treat the model’s internal confidence as proof of physical feasibility. The machine needs independent, real-time evidence from the plant and its surroundings to detect the divergence. Such evidence includes wheel-slip telemetry, disagreement between commanded and inertial-measurement-unit (IMU) acceleration, and motor current transients. The machine can then restrict motion using a response feasible under the updated traction estimate. If the machine has already consumed the clearance that response needs, detection can support harm mitigation but cannot establish collision avoidance.

Security threats target this same permission machinery by corrupting the evidence used to authorize motion. An adversary does not need to crack cryptographic keys or modify model weights to destabilize a physical agent; distorting the sensory inputs that inform admission decisions is sufficient. Projecting structured light patterns onto camera lenses, spoofing ultra-wideband (UWB) localization beacons, or injecting rogue packets into sensor buses can deceive the controller into approving destructive commands, even while the neural network executes its inference pipeline without numerical fault. Isolating the proposer from the permission path offers little defense if both subsystems ingest the same corrupted telemetry. Securing the causal boundary therefore requires validating the evidence itself. The validation consists of cross-checking heterogeneous sensor streams (for example, corroborating camera-based visual odometry against wheel encoders and inertial sensors), enforcing cryptographic message authentication across bus transceivers, and maintaining fail-operational plausibility checks that reject sensor readings deviating from physical conservation laws.

↪ A well-formed packet that carries a false measurement becomes a fault trial in the injection campaign developed in Physical sensor spoofing and proposer injection.

In digital systems, a cryptographic capability token or access control list entry grants authority to modify a file regardless of processor load or ambient conditions. In physical AI, physical authority is state-dependent. Cryptographic credentials establish identity and organizational rights to request an action, but physical admissibility depends entirely on the instantaneous operational state of the machine and its workspace. A joint velocity command of 2 rad/s may represent a routine transit when an industrial manipulator operates in open free space, yet that identical command becomes unacceptable if the arm is within 2 mm of a mechanical hard stop or a human coworker. Evaluating physical authority requires testing the proposed action against instantaneous kinematics, payload inertia, motor thermal headroom, and state estimation uncertainty. Admission control must also remain coupled to an executable fallback. If the permission gate rejects a proposed command because the machine approaches an operational boundary, the controller must immediately dispatch a dynamically feasible contingency maneuver, such as controlled regenerative braking or a compliant hold that yields to contact. The maneuver halts or redirects the plant without exceeding mechanical stress limits.

Accountability demands an audit trail that reconstructs the causal chain connecting sensory evidence to physical outcomes. When an embodied machine causes a collision or near-miss, post-incident analysis requires timestamp and clock-conversion uncertainty small enough to distinguish the relevant sensing, admission, dispatch, and physical-response events across multiple data streams. These streams include the raw observations ingested by the planner and the action proposed by the learned policy. They also include the state estimates and dynamic constraints evaluated by the admission gate, the filtered command dispatched to the motor drives, and the measured response from joint encoders and force-torque sensors. Command logs record the intended trajectory, while sensor telemetry measures the plant’s actual mechanical response. Recording dispatched setpoints alone does not confirm that an actuator executed them; an open-circuit fault, a tripped thermal breaker, or mechanical gearbox jamming will cause the physical plant to diverge immediately from commanded behavior. Both streams must be logged against synchronized hardware clocks (such as the IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol) so that investigators can distinguish between a planning failure, an admission gate error, and an electromechanical actuator breakdown. Reconstruction is necessary but does not by itself say who answers for an action. The decisions that set the machine’s envelope, its staffing, and its permission to operate are made before any tick, and they have to be recorded on purpose (Decisions Behind an Admitted Action).

↪ Protocol: The IEEE 1588 Precision Time Protocol exchange that aligns these clocks is developed in Clock Synchronization and Network Timestamps.

Safety, security, and accountability ultimately converge on three unified demands at the permission path guarding the causal boundary:

  1. Fresh, authenticated physical state: The permission gate must operate on telemetry fresh enough to bound physical uncertainty and robust enough against tampering or degradation to justify admitting an action.
  2. Deterministic authority to intervene: The admission mechanism needs uncompromised physical authority to veto or clamp infeasible proposals and inject safe contingency maneuvers, regardless of the learned model’s internal confidence or identity.
  3. Synchronized causal telemetry: The system must keep an immutable record of what was proposed, what state was evaluated, what commands were dispatched, and how the machine responded.

The incidents above map onto these demands. Tempe’s predictor discarded evidence it already had, and the factory emergency braking that could have intervened independently had been disabled; Cruise moved without fresh evidence about the space beneath it. Both investigations then depended on the third demand, the recorded telemetry from which the events were reconstructed. The permission path is therefore where a physical machine’s safety, security, and accountability obligations are discharged, and anchoring probabilistic proposals to checks against physical limits is what lets learned capability operate within justified bounds. What those checks must respect, and why, is the subject of four laws.

↪ The operational log that survives physical failure, from which an investigator reconstructs each admitted action, is developed in Trustworthy Operational Logs.

1.10 The Four Bedrock Laws

Every check on the permission path answers to constraints that neither a learned policy nor a software supervisor can negotiate away. Whether the warehouse machine is braking for a coworker in an aisle, pressing a spring-latched cage door, or carrying an unmodeled payload, it must respect the mechanical limits of its plant, adapt to dynamic environments altered by its own motion, and arbitrate between speculative model outputs and physically safe actuation. Four bedrock laws organize these engineering responsibilities, each anchoring a major part of this book:

  1. The first law: irreversibility. Software cannot roll back physical consequences. In pure digital systems, an erroneous transaction can be aborted and state restored from a checkpoint. In physical space, however, work performed on a plant is thermodynamic and permanent. A motor command that collides with a fixture, tears a cable, or fractures a fragile payload cannot be retracted. Every admissibility check must therefore complete before the machine consumes its remaining clearance margin. As the stopping distance budget demonstrates, processing latency directly expends physical clearance, forcing the mechanical actuators to brake over an ever-shrinking distance. Reversing a drive voltage may eventually return an arm to its starting coordinates, but it cannot mend cracked structural material or recover energy lost to friction and heat. Part I translates this physical reality into kinetic budgets for the robot’s body and deterministic timing constraints for its low-level controllers.
  2. The second law: endogenous data. In classic offline machine learning, data samples are drawn independently from a stationary distribution. In embodied systems, data generation is endogenous. Every control action alters the physical state and determines what sensors will observe next. If an arm nudges an object during an approach, the object shifts; if a mobile base drifts off its nominal path, the subsequent visual frames deviate from the training demonstrations. Single-step prediction accuracy on an offline benchmark cannot guarantee closed-loop behavior, because minor execution errors compound across the task horizon, the covariate shift met with the closed causal loop (section 1.4.1). Part II frames data collection, model training, and policy evaluation as a continuous improvement cycle: diagnose behavioral failures, curate targeted recovery demonstrations, retrain candidate policies, and systematically evaluate both performance gains and unintended regressions. Empirical evidence from past rollouts bounds what can be claimed about present capability; only qualified new experience can expand that envelope.
  3. The third law: proposal is not permission. A learned model operates only as a proposer, never as an unconstrained executor. An architecturally independent permission path (a runtime safety filter or an assurance monitor, for example) must arbitrate every proposed control command before that command reaches hardware actuators. Granting permission requires three prerequisites: fresh state estimates from verified sensors, an explicit check that the command respects every physical safety envelope, and an immediately executable fallback maneuver if the proposal is delayed, missing, or unsafe. This separation of authority must remain robust against system faults, so a runaway or stalled model thread must never be allowed to delay, starve, or corrupt the deterministic safety logic guarding the plant. Part III constructs the real-time execution runtime around this division between speculative proposal and independent, bounded permission.
  4. The fourth law: permission must be earned. An embodied machine earns permission to operate only as far as empirical evidence supports its performance under stated conditions. Flawless execution across a handful of laboratory trials does not justify autonomy across diverse surface friction, variable payloads, degraded lighting, or sensor degradation. Every deployment safety case must explicitly demarcate the system’s operational design domain (ODD),9 articulate the environmental assumptions underlying those boundaries, and specify fail-safe degradation policies whenever runtime conditions drift beyond verified bounds. The 2018 Tempe collision showed the cost of ignoring this boundary. The system architecture relied on an in-vehicle safety operator as the primary emergency countermeasure, yet lacked any mechanism to monitor whether the operator was actually attending to the roadway (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). Part IV formalizes command authority, fault injection testing, and release criteria so that epistemic and physical uncertainty bound what the machine is permitted to do.

9 Operational Design Domain (ODD): The automated-driving term for the stated environmental and operating conditions (roadway types, speed ranges, weather, lighting) under which a system is designed to work, and the starting scope of a UL 4600 safety case (UL Standards & Engagement 2023). This book applies the concept to the payloads, surface friction, operating speeds, and disturbances covered by the machine’s empirical testing. Operating outside that tested domain has no evidence behind it, which is why Choosing a tractable coverage table organizes the operating domain as an explicit scenario table against which collected training data are audited.

UL Standards & Engagement. 2023. ANSI/UL 4600 Ed. 3-2023: Standard for Evaluation of Autonomous Products. UL Standards & Engagement.

Of these four, only the first is a physical law. Irreversibility follows from Newtonian mechanics and thermodynamics. Work performed on a plant transfers energy and raises entropy, and no software architecture can undo that transition. The remaining three are engineering design principles, distilled from decades of systems engineering, industrial robotics, and aerospace failures. A team could build an architecture that violates them, for example by piping unvalidated network outputs straight to motor amplifiers, but it would incur severe and predictable risks. The book elevates all four to bedrock laws because it treats them as mandatory constraints for any production embodied system.

None of these three architectural principles is entirely novel. Each has a rich foundation in computer systems, control theory, and safety-critical engineering. Table 3 maps this book’s terminology to the established literature, alongside the canonical sources and documented real-world failures each principle distills, including the Tempe and Cruise incidents. Where the book’s terms introduce tighter operational precision, as when tying state freshness directly to the dynamic stopping budget, the book retains specialized systems phrasing. When searching broader engineering literature, the classical terms serve as the standard index.

The proposal boundary is where all four laws meet in one architecture. Irreversibility requires that every check complete before a command alters the plant, and endogenous data prevents offline training metrics from certifying a proposer in the closed loop. For these reasons, the third law (proposal is not permission) places that check on an independent permission path that the model cannot delay or corrupt. The fourth law (permission must be earned) then confines admitted proposals to the operating conditions that empirical evidence has verified. Scaling up model capacity does not relax these constraints; on the contrary, it intensifies physical pressure on the system. Although general machine learning methods deliver superior performance as compute expands (Sutton 2019), the running mobile machine must supply its onboard compute within a finite electrical and thermal budget. Every additional neural layer, attention head, or diffusion step draws against fixed physical allocations of memory bandwidth, electrical power, and thermal dissipation. Inference latency on the stopping path is travel that braking cannot recover. On the running machine at its drive limit, each millisecond costs 1.5 mm of clearance, as the stopping notebook showed. This compute, thermal, and temporal ceiling forms the physical wall confronting every learned proposer.

Sutton, Richard S. 2019. “The Bitter Lesson.” Incompleteideas.net 43.

↪ The memory-bandwidth and latency bottlenecks confronting onboard learned proposers are developed in The Cost of a Fresh Evaluation.

Finally, rejecting an inadmissible command must have an explicit physical realization. In traditional software, an unhandled exception or failed assertion can simply terminate a thread. On a moving robot, halting a software process leaves physical momentum untouched. A refusal is meaningful only if the execution runtime immediately dispatches an admissible fallback (a dynamic braking maneuver, a compliant hold, or a controlled stop) that the physical body can safely execute within its remaining stopping clearance. Translating the four laws and their runtime mechanisms into an engineered system takes the four parts of this book, each producing records that later parts rely on.

1.11 The Architecture of This Book

Moving a learned proposal, such as the running machine’s trajectory to open a warehouse cage door, from an abstract task requirement to safe, justified physical operation requires a disciplined four-stage progression. The engineer must first bound what the hardware plant can physically achieve and characterize what the learned proposer has demonstrated under rigorous testing. The engineer must then build an isolated runtime monitor to intercept live proposals and establish the operational conditions under which continued execution remains justified. Each stage corresponds to one of the four laws. The system of records illustrated in figure 9 formalizes this progression, capturing operating assumptions and empirical evidence so that downstream runtime decisions can inspect and rely on them. Conceptually, these records trace two converging pipelines. One strand establishes offline physical limits and verified competence; the second processes online sensor observations and real-time candidate actions. Both pipelines converge at the permission decision, where the system admits or rejects a proposed trajectory. In From a Better Prediction to Useful Service, this architecture synthesizes both strands to resolve the book’s central challenge of engineering dependable, high-performance physical systems out of unverified learned models and unforgiving mechanical hardware.

A record is a machine-checkable artifact that downstream safety and runtime decisions can verify and trust. The initial stopping calculation (notebook 1.1) already sketches the first record in the chain: a limit record, with the six elements the reader guide introduces (nominal value, operating conditions, uncertainty, margin, runtime detector, and provenance). Its nominal value is the derived stopping distance under stated operating conditions of loaded payload mass, dry concrete, a stationary obstacle, and constant approach speed. Because those inputs are illustrative scalars rather than calibrated distributions, however, its uncertainty remains unquantified. The apparent buffer between the obstacle and the machine is therefore only an arithmetic difference between provisional figures rather than a verified safety margin. The calculation also lacks a runtime detector (no sensor yet monitors whether floor traction drops or cargo shifts outside the assumed envelope, The six elements of a limit record), and its provenance rests on baseline assumptions rather than calibrated hardware logs. An ungrounded calculation becomes operational engineering evidence only when all six fields are populated and backed by empirical data. Every numbered box across the four part bands in figure 9 establishes a machine-checkable record of this rigor for a distinct architectural decision.

Part I, The Machine Anatomy, translates physical irreversibility into rigid architectural limits. The Physical Body profiles the mechanical plant to quantify its physical budgets (measurement freshness, stopping distance, deliverable torque, winding heat, and supply voltage). The Cognitive Brain measures the throughput and inference latency of the learned proposer to determine what it can deliver before deadlines expire. The Nervous System links compute to the actuators across deterministic buses, structuring a permission path whose timing guarantees derive from the plant’s stopping requirements. Together, the limit record and handoff record make every physical and temporal assumption explicit. Part II, Teaching the Machine, confronts the reality that physical actions continuously alter future sensor observations. Physical Data, Policy Training, and Closed-Loop Evaluation build the evidentiary chain required to collect demonstrations, train control policies, and bound policy performance to a declared operational design domain. Across this pipeline, the provenance record, policy manifest, and evaluation record document both the empirical competence demonstrated by the model and the operational boundaries where its statistical guarantees break down.

Part III, Running the Machine, enforces at runtime the separation between proposing an action and permitting it to execute. From Sensor Perception through Workload Placement, the chapters trace candidate commands through the online runtime. They qualify sensor measurements in observation contracts and maintain state estimates in a belief record. They bound a goal with an intent lease (a time-limited grant of a target, never of a path, and distinct from the chunk lease that bounds one motion segment). They check candidate trajectories against real-time physical constraints before admission and dispatch, then test whether the hardware keeps the permission path independent. Part IV, Governing the Machine, enforces the principle that permission must be earned. Supervisory Intervention, Adversarial Verification, and Deployment Release govern how authority transfers between the machine and a human operator, how fault injection tests whether protective responses hold, and how deployment decisions are assembled into release manifests. Responsible Physical AI then names who answers for each decision behind an admitted action. It also tests whether each human role the design relies on is one a person can perform, and it counts the people who bear the machine’s risk and appear in its records. Finally, Engineering Physical AI brings the framework to bear on an unfamiliar machine, synthesizing what an engineering team can construct and formally justify while identifying the residual risks that require further mitigation. Across these parts, the warehouse mobile manipulator introduced here provides a single concrete machine on which to ground each architectural decision. Its records render design assumptions fully inspectable, though an engineer must remember that assembling a record documents an explicit claim rather than guaranteeing its physical truth. The system record register in The record register catalogs all sixteen records, noting their producing chapters and the specific invariants they bind, while the machine card in section 1.4.2 specifies the foundational physical parameters of the robot itself. Together, this four-stage route forms a repeatable discipline for making learned behavior dependable in physical space, guarding against the common misconceptions that arise when digital assumptions are carried across the causal boundary.

1.12 Fallacies and Pitfalls

The misconceptions that follow arise when assumptions from pure software engineering, statistical learning, or classical control theory are imported into embodied machines. In digital systems, an erroneous state can typically be trapped, rolled back, or restarted. Across the causal boundary, however, software outputs become physical forces that alter the world irrevocably. Preventing physical harm requires hard deterministic guarantees that learning objectives, human operators, and asynchronous recovery routines cannot provide on their own.

Fallacy: Safety constraints can be trained into the policy.

Engineering teams often attempt to enforce physical safety by adding penalty terms, barrier penalties, or negative rewards to the training objective. While penalty shaping suppresses undesirable actions over the training distribution, it provides no mathematical guarantee on the actions emitted during deployment. Deep neural networks remain uncalibrated function approximators when exposed to out-of-distribution inputs, sensor degradation, or novel environmental dynamics. A statistical loss function optimizes expected performance, but safety requires hard invariants that hold on every control cycle. For this reason, deterministic safety enforcement must sit downstream of the policy, operating as an independent permission path that filters proposed commands before they reach actuator driver registers, exactly where the third law places the boundary gate.

Pitfall: Treating human supervisory intervention as instantaneous in shared-autonomy systems.

In shared-autonomy designs, such as a robotic surgical tool providing haptic guidance from a learned anatomical model, system designers sometimes treat the human operator as a failsafe catch-all. This assumption ignores the physiological limits of human reaction time, which ranges between 150 ms and 250 ms for simple tactile or visual reflexes and grows under cognitive load. Once console video and a handshake are added, the running machine’s card budgets an in-loop takeover at 605 ms. Over that interval, an actuator running on a 1 kHz control loop can execute hundreds of unchecked cycles, generating enough force to tear soft tissue or damage a mechanical workpiece before the operator can intervene. Delegating authority to a human supervisor does not eliminate the machine’s capacity for harm during the supervisory lag. The permission path must enforce local force, velocity, and displacement envelopes autonomously, preserving the established physical envelopes under their operating assumptions without relying on timely human overrides.

Pitfall: Assuming software latency merely degrades performance gracefully.

In purely digital systems, inference latency or scheduler jitter increases response time or degrades service quality. In physical systems, timing is a functional correctness requirement. If a learned policy stalls, whether from accelerator kernel contention, memory allocation pauses, or network serialization delays, allowing its previous command to persist across subsequent control cycles turns stale intent into physical danger. For example, a mobile manipulator that repeats its last forward-velocity command during a 100 ms inference hiccup can collide with an unmapped obstacle. Actuation commands must therefore carry bounded temporal validity, enforced by hardware or real-time software watchdogs. When that validity window (the chunk lease of the stopping budget) expires without a verified update, the low-level controller must begin a fallback within its established response budget. The selected stop or hold must remain feasible for the current mechanism, state, and surviving resources.

Fallacy: Assuming physical actuation is reversible through subsequent software compensation.

Transactional software architectures rely on rollbacks and compensation routines to undo erroneous operations. In embodied systems, thermodynamics and materials science break this equivalence. If a robotic arm applies excessive torque to a workpiece, issuing a reverse motor command cannot unbend plastically deformed metal, repair fractured composite, or recover the electrical energy dissipated as heat in the motor windings (\(\MotorPhaseRmsCurrent^2 \PhaseResistance\)). Anomaly detection routines that react after physical contact can only truncate ongoing damage; they cannot undo the mechanical work already transferred across the causal boundary. Preventive verification, such as checking proposed trajectories against workspace boundaries, torque limits, and kinetic energy caps before dispatch, must serve as the primary defense. Reactive recovery strategies remain necessary for unexpected disturbances, but they function as a secondary backstop rather than a substitute for preventive enforcement.

1.13 Summary

Physical AI converges two historically distinct engineering disciplines: classical robotics and modern machine learning. As learned models move progressively closer to direct actuator control, the boundary between digital computation and physical reality sharpens at the causal boundary, the interface where an admitted command becomes an irreversible physical effect. In purely software systems, state rollbacks and retries can recover from algorithmic faults; in the physical world, software cannot undo kinetic energy, momentum, or impact. Safe recovery requires an executable, physically feasible countermeasure.

Physical dynamics also do not pause while neural networks compute. Because a machine continues to travel during sensing and processing delays, safety requires deterministic admission checks upstream of any consequential actuation. As the warehouse mobile manipulator’s stopping budget and the Tempe collision show, prediction quality offers no protection once response delay has spent the clearance. The architecture resolves this tension by separating proposal from permission and giving an independent, bounded-latency permission path the authority to refuse. Four laws, one physical and three design principles, organize the framework the rest of the book develops, while the elementary stopping budget and its crossover speed form this chapter’s quantitative core (table 4).

Key Takeaways: Engineering across the causal boundary
  • Useful behavior requires an integrated machine: Physical AI combines learned decision-making, physical dynamics, and delegated mechanical authority. Delivering reliable autonomous operation requires co-designing task competence with physical body limits, real-time compute runtimes, and the operational evidence supporting deployment.
  • Response delay consumes physical margin: The elementary stopping budget \(\StoppingDistance = \SpeedAtDetection\PrebrakeDelay + \SpeedAtDetection^2/(2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration) \le \ClearDistance\) partitions stopping distance into latency-induced travel and mechanical braking. Below the crossover speed \(\StoppingCrossoverSpeed = 2\CredibleBrakingDeceleration\PrebrakeDelay\), response delay dominates stopping distance; above \(\StoppingCrossoverSpeed\), braking deceleration dominates. For the warehouse mobile manipulator at its drive limit, pre-braking travel adds 35.6 percent to braking distance. The decisive delay belongs to whichever path triggers the protective response, which can operate independently of the learned proposer’s inference time.
  • Proposal and permission serve distinct architectural roles: The learned proposer (the Brain) generates goal-directed actions, while an independent permission path admits, modifies, or rejects them before they cross the causal boundary. Safety enforcement relies on validated sensor inputs, physical kinematic envelopes, strict real-time deadlines, and verifiable fallback maneuvers.
  • Evidence governs safe operation and systematic refinement: Four laws structure this book. Irreversibility is a physical law; the other three (endogenous data, proposal is not permission, and permission must be earned) are design principles that translate covariate shift, runtime assurance, and the assurance case into systems practice. Together they link physical hardware, embodied learning, real-time execution, and operational governance, establishing verified service within defined operational envelopes while identifying the targeted evaluations needed to expand them.

Before judging whether a proposed action is right, an engineer should establish what physical response would remain if the proposal were wrong, stale, or never arrived, and whether the machine still holds the clearance, the time, and the authority to carry that response out. A design that cannot answer from its own measured limits has handed safety to prediction quality, and the stopping budget showed that prediction quality cannot buy back clearance once delay has spent it. The answer therefore starts from what the plant can do under stated conditions, never from what the model believes.

1.14 Historical Perspective and Further Reading

In physical artificial intelligence, moving learned models directly into the actuation loop creates severe timing and safety hazards. The engineering principles that resolve these challenges originate in embedded systems, real-time control, and safety engineering. General-purpose computing treats execution time as a mere performance optimization, assuming that correctness depends solely on logical input-output relationships. Cyber-physical systems theory overturned this assumption, holding that physical dynamics and computational processes must be modeled jointly because a command dispatched at the wrong time is physically incorrect (Lee 2008). This insight forms the foundation of the causal boundary. Similarly, separating command proposal from execution permission stems from runtime assurance architectures. The Simplex architecture demonstrated that an industrial control system could safely run an unverified, high-performance controller if paired with a certified baseline controller and deterministic switching logic capable of taking back the plant before safety boundaries were violated (Seto et al. 1998; Sha 2001). Modern safety filters in embodied machine learning generalize this design, validating neural network proposals against mathematical certificates of safe states (control barrier functions or reachability envelopes, which Safety Enforcement develops) before granting actuator permission (Hsu et al. 2024).

Seto, Danbing, Bruce Krogh, Lui Sha, and Alongkrit Chutinan. 1998. “The Simplex Architecture for Safe Online Control System Upgrades.” Proceedings of the 1998 American Control Conference (ACC) 6: 3504–8. https://doi.org/10.1109/ACC.1998.703255.
Hsu, Kai-Chieh, Haimin Hu, and Jaime F. Fisac. 2024. “The Safety Filter: A Unified View of Safety-Critical Control in Autonomous Systems.” Annual Review of Control, Robotics, and Autonomous Systems 7: 47–72. https://doi.org/10.1146/annurev-control-071723-102940.
Kelly, Timothy Patrick. 1998. “Arguing Safety: A Systematic Approach to Managing Safety Cases.” PhD thesis, Department of Computer Science, University of York.

Many foundational safety principles in physical systems emerged from forensic investigations of catastrophic failures. The Therac-25 radiation therapy disasters demonstrated the fatal peril of replacing mechanical interlocks with software routines (Leveson and Turner 1993). When race conditions bypassed software checks, the machine administered lethal radiation overdoses, proving that software reliability does not imply system safety. Systems-theoretic safety subsequently recast accidents not as isolated component breakdowns, but as failures of the overall control structure to enforce safety constraints on the interactions among components, feedback loops, and human operators (Leveson 2011). This shift led directly to the assurance case, a structured argument demonstrating that concrete evidence satisfies defined safety claims within explicit operating environments (Kelly 1998). The UL 4600 standard adopted this structure as its central framework for autonomous products (UL Standards & Engagement 2023), abandoning arbitrary road-testing mileage in favor of verifiable, claim-based arguments. Public accident records, particularly the National Transportation Safety Board investigation of the 2018 Tempe autonomous vehicle collision (National Transportation Safety Board 2019), remain the field’s most complete documentation of how safety arguments unravel when classification changes, a response delay chosen in software, and reliance on a human fallback interact in the field.

  • Cyber-physical systems (Lee 2008). Lee articulates why classical computing abstractions fail when computation interacts with physical processes. General-purpose computer architectures deliberately abstract away time, treating execution speed as a performance consideration rather than a correctness specification. Lee demonstrates that for physical interactions, the timing of an actuation command is inseparable from its physical meaning, providing the conceptual foundation for treating the causal boundary as an explicit systems interface.
  • Runtime assurance (Sha 2001). Sha’s paper on the Simplex architecture is the place to see how the switching logic decides when to take the plant back from the unverified controller, and why that logic must be simpler than what it supervises. It is the pattern behind this book’s separation of proposal from permission.
  • Therac-25 (Leveson and Turner 1993). Leveson and Turner’s forensic analysis traces the specific race conditions and the development and reporting practices that let the overdoses recur, detail the summary above omits. It remains the essential case study in why protective functions need justification independent of the software they supervise.
  • Systems-theoretic safety (Leveson 2011). Leveson introduces the STAMP accident model, reframing safety as a continuous control problem rather than a collection of component failure probabilities. Accidents occur when the control structure fails to enforce safety constraints across component interactions, sensor delays, and feedback loops. This text provides the theoretical basis for enforcing safety invariants as dynamic runtime constraints rather than static design assumptions.
  • The Tempe investigation (National Transportation Safety Board 2019). The National Transportation Safety Board report on the fatal 2018 Tempe autonomous vehicle crash offers the most thorough empirical record of an embodied AI safety failure. The report details how classification changes that discarded tracking history, a one-second action suppression meant to limit responses to false alarms, the separate deactivation of the vehicle’s factory emergency braking over possible radar interference, and reliance on an operator whose attention nothing monitored combined in the collision. The event timeline and sensor telemetry provide vital reading for anyone building real-time perception-action loops.
Lee, Edward A. 2008. “Cyber Physical Systems: Design Challenges.” Proceedings of the 11th IEEE International Symposium on Object and Component-Oriented Real-Time Distributed Computing (ISORC), 363–69. https://doi.org/10.1109/ISORC.2008.25.
Sha, Lui. 2001. “Using Simplicity to Control Complexity.” IEEE Software 18 (4): 20–28.
Leveson, Nancy G., and Clark S. Turner. 1993. “An Investigation of the Therac-25 Accidents.” Computer 26 (7): 18–41.
Leveson, Nancy. 2011. Engineering a Safer World: Systems Thinking Applied to Safety. MIT Press.

What’s Next: From the causal boundary to physical limits
A permission decision needs a physical limit against which to judge the proposed action. The Physical Body begins Part I with the five budgets the permission path must check before it commits torque (measurement freshness, stopping distance, deliverable torque, winding heat, and supply voltage) and asks which binds first for a given maneuver, under what stress, and on what evidence. Each budget ends as a limit record, the six-element form this chapter sketched from its stopping calculation. The Cognitive Brain then examines the learned proposer’s capabilities and timing, and The Nervous System uses both sets of constraints to design the handoff and independent permission path. The progression starts with what the machine can sustain and ends with how computation may safely request it.
